Deformations and lifts of Calabi–Yau varieties in characteristic p p
Lukas Brantner, Lenny Taelman

Abstract
. We study deformations of Calabi–Yau varieties in characteristic p p using techniques from derived algebraic geometry. We prove a mixed characteristic analogue of the Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem (which states that Calabi–Yau varieties in characteristic 0 0 are unobstructed), and we show that ordinary Calabi–Yau varieties admit canonical lifts to characteristic 0 0 , generalising the Serre–Tate theorem on ordinary abelian varieties.



1.
Introduction

We call a smooth and proper variety
X

Calabi–Yau
if its canonical bundle
\omega_{X}
is trivial.

The Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem
[
9
,
63
,
64
]
asserts that (formal) deformations of complex
Calabi–Yau varieties are unobstructed, equivalently, that their Kuranishi spaces are smooth.

In characteristic
p
, this result is generally false: Hirokado constructed the first obstructed Calabi–Yau threefolds in
[
28
]
, and
numerous other examples have been discovered since
[
19
,
60
,
4
,
13
]
.
Some of these fail to lift to characteristic
0
, while others even have obstructed equal characteristic deformations. Counterexamples do not just occur in ‘small characteristic’ — indeed, Cynk–van Straten’s obstructed threefolds
[
13
]
live in characteristic up to
p=9001
.

Our principal aim is to prove that,
in spite of these examples, many
Calabi–Yau varieties in characteristic
p
have unobstructed mixed characteristic deformations. In the ordinary case, we will even construct canonical lifts to characteristic zero, thereby generalising classical Serre–Tate theory for ordinary abelian varieties.

To prove these results, we analyse the effect of derived period maps between formal moduli problems on (Koszul dual) tangent fibres.

1.1.
Statement of Results

We begin by summarising our main results.

Unobstructedness

First, we establish the following mixed characteristic analogue of the classical Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem:

Theorem A

(Unobstructedness)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
with ring of Witt vectors
W(k)
.
Let
X
be a smooth, proper, and geometrically irreducible scheme over
k
with trivial canonical bundle. Assume that

(1)

the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence for
X
degenerates at
E_{1}
;

(2)

the crystalline cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/W(k))
is torsion-free.

Then the mixed characteristic formal deformations of
X
are unobstructed.

Remark 1.1
.

Our proof in fact gives unobstructedness under slightly weaker hypotheses; we refer to
Theorem

7.16
of the main text for a sharper statement.

If
\dim(X)\leq p
, then condition (
1
) in
Theorem

A
is necessary by the work of Deligne–Illusie
[
16
]
. We do not know of an example of a variety with trivial canonical bundle and degenerating Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence that has obstructed deformations (and hence violates condition (
2
)).

Ekedahl–Shepherd-Barron
[
18
]

and Schröer
[
59
]
have shown related unobstructedness theorems,
albeit under more restrictive hypotheses. The main
difference is that they
assume
the existence of a lift to characteristic
0
to prove unobstructedness.
Derived deformation theory allows us to eliminate such a condition.

Serre–Tate coordinates

Our second main theorem is a generalisation of classical Serre–Tate theory for ordinary abelian varieties. First, let us recall some notation:

Definition 1.2

(Bloch–Kato ordinary)
.

Let
X
be smooth and proper variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p
.
Denote the image of

d\colon\Omega^{r-1}_{X/k}\rightarrow\Omega^{r}_{X/k}
by
B^{r}_{X/k}
.
Then
X
is said to be
Bloch–Kato
n
-ordinary
if
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X,B^{r}_{X/k})\cong 0
for all
r\leq n
, and
Bloch–Kato ordinary
if it is
n
-ordinary for all
n
.

Remark 1.3
.

Assume that the first
n
slopes of the Newton and the Hodge polygon of
X
agree, which means that for all
i
and all
r\leq n-1
, we have

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\left(K\otimes_{W(k)}\mathrm{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/W(k))\right)^{F=p^{r}}=\dim_{k}\mathrm{H}^{i-r}(X,\Omega^{r}_{X})

where
K=W(\overline{k})[1/p]
and
F
is the Frobenius.
Then
X
is Bloch–Kato
n
-ordinary. If
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/W)
is torsion–free, the
converse holds true.

Remark 1.4
.

An abelian variety or K3 surface is Bloch–Kato
1
-ordinary iff it is Bloch–Kato ordinary, which happens precisely if it is
ordinary in the classical sense.

Theorem B

(Serre–Tate coordinates)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
, with ring of Witt vectors
W(k)
.
Let
X
be a smooth, proper, and geometrically irreducible scheme over
k
of dimension
d
and with
\omega_{X/k}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
such that

(1)

X
is Bloch–Kato
2
-ordinary;

(2)

the group
\mathrm{H}^{d}(X_{\bar{k},\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}},\,\mathbf{Z}_{p})
is torsion-free.

Then the deformation functor of
X
is canonically isomorphic to a formal group
\ST_{X}
of multiplicative type over
\Spf W(k)
.

Remark 1.5
.

If the first two slopes of the Hodge and the Newton polygon of
X
agree, then
X
automatically satisfies conditions (1) and (2). If
X
is Bloch-Kato
2
-ordinary and
\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
vanishes, then condition (
2
) is automatically satisfied.

A formal group
T
is of multiplicative type if
it is étale locally isomorphic to a finite product of groups of the form
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{m}
and
\mu_{p^{n}}
. The formal group is then determined by its character group
\Hom(T_{\bar{k}},\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{m,\bar{k}})
, which is a finite
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-module equipped with a continuous
\Gal({\bar{k}/k})
-action.

We give an explicit description of the formal torus in
Remark

8.30
. If the character group is torsion-free, it also follows that deformations of
X
are unobstructed.

Remark 1.6
.

Theorem

B
recovers results of Serre–Tate
[
61
,
33
]
for abelian varieties, and Deligne
[
14
]
and Nygaard
[
50
]
for K3 surfaces.

Achinger and Zdanowicz
[
4
]
have shown a result related to
Theorem

B
under more restrictive hypotheses. The main difference is that they
assume
that deformations of
X
are unobstructed, and in particular that
X
is already known to lift to
W(k)
. Our proof is inspired by their proof. The principal novelty in our approach is again the use of derived deformation theory, which allows us to eliminate the condition that
X
is unobstructed.

Theorem

B
immediately implies the following result:

Corollary 1.7

(Canonical lifts)
.

Given
X/k
as in
Theorem

B
,
X

admits a
canonical
formal lift
X^{\mathrm{can}}
to
W(k)
corresponding to the unit section of
\ST_{X}
.

This formal lift is often algebraisable:

Theorem C

(Algebraisation)
.

Let
X/k
be as in
Theorem

B
. If
X
is projective, then so is its canonical lift
X^{\mathrm{can}}
.

In fact, we show that
for
X/k
as in
Theorem

B
and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle on
X
, the
deformations of the pair
(X,\mathcal{L})
are also parametrised by a formal group
\ST_{X,\mathcal{L}}
of multiplicative type, see

Theorem

8.28
.

Remark 1.8
.

Unlike the case of ordinary abelian varieties or K3 surfaces, we generally do not expect the canonical lifts
X^{\mathrm{can}}
to be of CM type, see
Remark

8.34
.

Classical Serre–Tate theory admits an ‘orthogonal generalisation’ to
abelian varieties over connective
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-rings,
which is a key ingredient to Lurie’s moduli-theoretic interpretation of topological modular forms and Morava
E
-theory
[
35
,
36
]
.

In this context, there is a
p
-complete
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
\SW(k)
, the
spherical Witt vectors
, which is flat over the sphere and satisfies

\pi_{0}(\SW(k))\cong W(k).

It is then natural to ask if our canonical lifts
X^{\mathrm{can}}
can further be lifted to
\SW(k)
. We show that this rarely happens:

Theorem D
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective irreducible scheme over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p
. Assume
\omega_{X/k}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
. If
X
admits a lift to a formal spectral scheme over the spherical Witt vectors
\SW(k)
, then
X
has a finite étale cover by an ordinary abelian variety.

1.2.
Outline of proofs

Before outlining our proofs in characteristic
p
, we will briefly trace
through the history of the
Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem in characteristic
0
, and review a derived proof in this regime.

Background on the BTT theorem

The unobstructedness of complex Calabi–Yau varieties was first established by Tian
[
63
]
and
Todorov
[
64
]
, building on Bogomolov’s work
[
9
]
on holomorphic symplectic manifolds.

Goldman and Milson
[
22
]
later reinterpreted Tian–Todorov’s transcendental argument
in terms of differential graded Lie algebras, using that these control formal deformations in characteristic
0
.
This paradigm had been proposed shortly before by Deligne
[
15
]
and Drinfel’d
[
17
]
. It was later
promoted by Lurie
[
41
]
and Pridham
[
54
]
to an equivalence between derived formal deformation functors, so-called
formal moduli problems
, and differential graded Lie algebras.

The first algebraic proof of the Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem in characteristic
0
was given by Ran
[
57
]
.
Iacono and Manetti
[
30
]

(cf. also
[
20
]
)
later found a purely algebraic proof using differential graded Lie algebras, in the spirit of Goldman–Milson.

Unobstructedness in characteristic
0

We briefly sketch a proof of unobstructedness in characteristic
0
.
Rather than considering deformations of
X
over local Artinian
k
-algebras, one deforms
X
over
simplicial (i.e. animated) local Artinian
k
-algebras
R
.
Given such an
R
, let us write

\Def_{X}(R)\in\mathscr{S}

for the space (i.e.
\infty
-groupoid) of derived schemes
\mathfrak{X}/R
lifting
X
to
R
.
The functor
R\mapsto\Def(R)
is an example of a
formal moduli problem
, i.e. a functor satisfying a derived version of the Schlessinger axioms (cf.
Definition

3.6
).

One advantage of formal moduli problems
F
is that they carry a canonical and functorial obstruction theory.
This means that if
R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow R
is an elementary extension of classical local Artinian rings with kernel
I
and
x\in\pi_{0}F(R)
, then there is a canonical element
\ob(x,R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow R)
in
\pi_{0}F(\sqz_{k}(I[1]))
which vanishes if and only if
x
lifts to
\pi_{0}F(R^{\prime})
. Here
\sqz_{k}(I[n]))=k\oplus I[n]
is the trivial square-zero extension of
k
by
I[n]
. For
I=k
, we obtain the ‘shifted dual numbers’.

The spaces
F(\sqz_{k}(k[0])),F(\sqz_{k}(k[1])),F(\sqz_{k}(k[2])),\ldots
assemble to a spectrum
T_{F}
,
the
tangent fibre
of
F
, which can be promoted to a chain

complex over
k
.

In fact,
T_{F}[-1]
can even be promoted to a differential graded Lie algebra, which is Koszul dual to
F
.
One can then recover the formal moduli problem
F
from
T_{F}
: the value
F(R)
is given by the space of maps
\mathfrak{D}(R)\rightarrow T_{F}
, where
\mathfrak{D}(R)
is the differential graded Lie algebra Koszul dual to
R
, i.e. its André–Quillen cohomology.

For
F=\Def_{X}
, we obtain the Kodaira–Spencer Lie algebra

T_{\Def_{X}}=R\Gamma(X,T_{X})[1]

with the commutator bracket of vector fields,
and we have

\pi_{0}\Def_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))\cong\pi_{-n}T_{\Def_{X}}\cong\mathrm{H}^{n+1}(X,T_{X}).

This derived perspective gives rise to a conceptually clear proof the Bogomolov–Tian–Todorov theorem, which we briefly sketch for
k=\mathbf{C}
.

First, one constructs a map of formal moduli problems

\per_{X}\colon\Def_{X/\mathbf{C}}\longrightarrow\Per_{X/\mathbf{C}},

which is a (formal) derived version of the classical period map à la Griffiths. It measures how the first part of the Hodge filtration varies when
X
moves in a family. More precisely, the functor
\per_{X}
sends a deformation
\mathfrak{X}/R
to the morphism

\Fil^{1}\RGamma_{\dR}(\mathfrak{X}/R)\longrightarrow\RGamma_{\dR}(\mathfrak{X}/R)\simeq R\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\RGamma(X(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{Z}).

Here the equivalence is an avatar of the Gauss–Manin connection.

If
\omega_{X}
is trivial, then one checks that the linear map

(1)

\pi_{0}\Def_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\Per_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))

is injective for all
n\geq 0
.

For
n=0
, this is the tangent to the classical period map; its injectivity is the infinitesimal Torelli theorem for Calabi–Yau varieties. For
n=1
, we see that obstruction classes for deformations of
X
map injectively to
obstruction classes for
\Per_{X}
.

The period domain
\Per_{X}
is a kind of ‘derived Grassmannian’, and one easily verifies that it is unobstructed. It then follows from (
1
) that the obstruction classes for deformations of
X
must always vanish.

In fact,
\Per_{X}
is even ‘derived unobstructed’, i.e. pro-represented by a free
commutative differential graded algebra. This is equivalent to the corresponding differential graded Lie algebra being abelian (as its homotopy groups are finite-dimensional).

Using that
\pi_{i}\Def_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))\longrightarrow\pi_{i}\Per_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))

is injective for all
i
and all
n\geq 0
, one can show that
X
is also derived unobstructed. Indeed, one can postcompose the induced map on tangent fibres
T_{\Def_{X}}\rightarrow T_{\Per_{X}}
with a map to an abelian differential graded Lie algebra such that the composite is an equivalence. This argument is closely related to the proof of Iacono–Manetti
[
30
]
, and its interpretation due to Fiorenza–Martinengo
[
20
]
.

Formal moduli problems in mixed characteristic

The theory of formal moduli problems and their tangent fibres admits a well-behaved generalisation to mixed characteristic
[
11
]
. When
k
is a perfect field of characteristic
p
, formal moduli problems
F
are now defined on animated local Artinian
W(k)
-algebras with
residue field
k
.
Their tangent fibres
T_{F}
are still chain complexes over
k
, which can be equipped with
partition Lie algebra structures controlling the formal moduli problems
F
.

In particular, there is a formal moduli problem
\Def_{X/W}
encoding the mixed characteristic derived infinitesimal deformations of a given variety
X
over
k
.

When attempting to adapt the above proof to the mixed characteristic setting, one however encounters a fundamental obstacle first observed by Grothendieck
[
24
]
: the de Rham cohomology is no longer rigid, as the
Gauss–Manin connection generally need not exist.
The above definition of the period map therefore no longer makes sense.
We can circumvent this issue in two ways:

(1)

Construct a period map for deformations of
X
over animated local Artinian rings
with divided powers
on their maximal ideals;

(2)

Construct a period map based on the unit root part of crystalline cohomology of
X
, which is an infinitesimal crystal (cf.
[
51
, Cor.4.11]
).

The first approach leads to a proof of our Unobstructedness
Theorem

A
, while the second leads to a proof of our Serre–Tate
Theorem

B
.

Unobstructedness in mixed characteristic

Let
R\rightarrow k
be a local Artinian animated ring equipped with a divided power structure on its augmentation ideal (in the sense of Mao
[
47
]
). For every lift
\mathfrak{X}/R
,
the derived de Rham cohomology
\RGamma_{\dR}(\mathfrak{X}/R)
agrees with the crystalline cohomology
\RGamma_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/R)
.
We can therefore
send
\mathfrak{X}/R
to

\Fil^{1}\RGamma_{\dR}(\mathfrak{X}/R)\longrightarrow\RGamma_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/R)\simeq R\otimes_{W(k)}\RGamma_{\mathrm{cris}}(X/W(k)),

and this assignment defines a map of ‘divided power formal moduli problems’

\per^{\mathrm{pd}}_{X}\colon\Def^{\mathrm{pd}}_{X/W}\longrightarrow\Per^{\mathrm{pd}}_{X/W}.

Here
\Def^{\mathrm{pd}}_{X/W}
and
\Per^{\mathrm{pd}}_{X/W}
are obtained by restricting corresponding genuine formal moduli problems
\Def_{X/W}
and
\Per_{X/W}
to animated rings with divided powers, and
\Per_{X/W}
is again a kind of ‘derived Grassmannian’.

Under conditions (
1
) and (
2
) in
Theorem

A
, we show that the induced maps

\pi_{0}\Def_{X}^{\mathrm{pd}}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\Per_{X}^{\mathrm{pd}}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))

are injective for all
n\geq 0
and that and that
\Per_{X/W}
is unobstructed. From this, we deduce that deformations of
X
are unobstructed along small maps
f\colon R^{\prime}\rightarrow R
of local Artinian divided power rings with the property that the divided powers vanish on
\ker(f)
.
Following an idea of Schröer
[
59
]
and using a result of Achinger–Suh
[
2
]
, we then bootstrap from this partial unobstructedness to show that
\Def_{X}
is unobstructed on all local Artinian (commutative) rings with residue
field
k
.

Remark 1.9
.

For this bootstrapping argument, it is crucial to consider mixed characteristic deformations, even if one is only interested in unobstructedness along maps of
k
-algebras.

Serre–Tate theory for ordinary Calabi–Yau varieties

Another advantage of derived deformation theory is that it is easy to detect when a map
F\rightarrow G
of formal moduli problems is an equivalence, as the tangent fibre functor is conservative. This means that
F\rightarrow G
is an equivalence if and only if
the induced map on tangent fibres
T_{F}\rightarrow T_{G}
is an equivalence, i.e. if
for all
n
and
i
, the induced map

\pi_{i}F(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))\longrightarrow\pi_{i}G(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))

is an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces.

This observation is key to our proof of
Theorem

B
. We briefly outline our construction for
k
algebraically closed, and refer to the main text for the general case.

Given a Bloch–Kato

1
-ordinary Calabi–Yau variety
X
over
k
and a lift
\mathfrak{X}
to an animated local Artinian ring
R
with residue field
k
, we construct a morphism

R\Gamma(X,W\Omega_{X}^{1})^{F=1}\longrightarrow H^{d}(X,W\mathcal{O}_{X})^{F=1}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{m}(R)[1-d]

Varying
R
, these arrows assemble to a map of formal moduli problems

\st_{X}\colon\Def_{X/W}\longrightarrow\ST_{X},

where
\ST_{X}
has the structure of a (derived) formal group of multiplicative type.
This is a derived version of a construction of Nygaard
[
50
]
and Achinger–Zdanowicz
[
4
]
.

We then prove that if
X
is Bloch–Kato
2
-ordinary, the induced maps

\pi_{i}\st_{X}(\sqz_{k}(k[n]))

are isomorphisms for all
i,n\geq 0
, which implies that
\Def_{X/W}
is equivalent to
\ST_{X}
by conservativity of the tangent fibre.

1.3.

\backslash
R1

2.
Preliminaries

To set the stage, we briefly recall the cotangent complex formalism of André
[
5
]
and Quillen
[
55
]
in the level of generality needed for our later applications.

2.1.
Adjoining sifted colimits

Homological algebra replaces modules by chain complexes to refine classical functors like the tensor product by more refined constructions like Tor groups.
Quillen’s homotopical algebra
[
56
]
refines functors defined on categories which are not abelian, e.g. the category of commutative rings.

Higher category theory gives a clean construction of the required nonabelian derived
\infty
-categories. Indeed, let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\infty
-category with finite coproducts, and write
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
for the
\infty
-category of functors
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
which preserve finite products.
By
[
44
, Section 5.5.8]
, the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
is
freely generated by
\mathscr{C}
under sifted colimits. This means that
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
admits sifted colimits and for
every other
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
with sifted colimits, precomposing with the
(fully faithful) Yoneda embedding
j\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})

gives an equivalence

\Fun_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C}),\mathscr{D})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Fun(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}).

Here
\Fun_{\Sigma}
is the full subcategory of functors that preserve sifted colimits.

Hence every functor
f\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
to an
\infty
-category with sifted colimits has an essentially unique sifted-colimit-preserving extension
F\colon\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
.

Example 2.1

(Connective chain complexes)
.

For
\mathscr{C}=\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\ff}\subset\Ab
the full subcategory of finite free abelian groups,
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category
\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{cn}}

of connective module spectra over the Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum of
\mathbf{Z}
, which is in turn equivalent to the derived
\infty
-category

\mathscr{D}_{\geq 0}(\mathbf{Z})
of connective chain complexes (cf.
[
43
, Remark 7.1.1.16, Corollary 7.1.4.15.]
).
The full subcategory of discrete objects is equivalent to the ordinary category
\Ab
of abelian groups.

Proposition 2.2
.

The functor
\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\ff}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
A
to the underlying set extends to a sifted-colimit-preserving functor
\Omega^{\infty}:\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{cn}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

which is conservative and creates small limits.

We recall some basic properties of
the
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}
-construction (cf.
[
44
, Proposition 5.5.8.10]
,
[
44
, Lemma 5.5.8.14]
):

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a small
\infty
-category that admits finite coproducts. Then

(1)

\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
is presentable;

(2)

for every
x\in\mathscr{C}
the functor
\Map_{\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})}(x,-)
preserves sifted colimits;

(3)

every object in
\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C})
can be written as a sifted colimit of objects in
\mathscr{C}
.

2.2.
Animated commutative rings

Let us write
\CRing
for the ordinary category of (commutative) rings, and
\Poly\subset\CRing
for its full subcategory spanned by polynomial rings
\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

Definition 2.4

(Animated rings)
.

The
\infty
-category of
animated (commutative) rings

\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}:=\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\Poly)
is obtained by freely adjoining sifted colimits to
\Poly
.

Equivalently,
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
can be obtained by
inverting the weak equivalences in the category of simplicial commutative rings with its Quillen model structure. We briefly review the basic properties of
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
, referring to
[
45
, Chapter 25]
for details.

By
[
45
, Remark 25.1.1.4]
, the full subcategory
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\heartsuit}\subset\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
spanned by all discrete objects is equivalent to the ordinary category of commutative rings.

The
\infty
-category
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
is presentable; hence, it admits small limits and colimits. As every object in
\Poly
is a coproduct of finitely many
copies of
\mathbf{Z}[x]
, we have:

Proposition 2.5

(Forgetful functor)
.

The functor
\Poly\rightarrow\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{cn}}
sending a polynomial ring to its underlying abelian group extends to a
sifted-colimit-preserving functor

U\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{cn}}

which is conservative and it creates small limits and sifted colimits.

Given
A\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
, write
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A}:=\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A/}
the
\infty
-category of maps
A\rightarrow B
.

Construction 2.6

(
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A}
as functor of
A
)
.

Consider the
\infty
-category

\mathscr{C}:=\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}).

The functor
q\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
,
(A\rightarrow B)\mapsto A
is evidently a cartesian fibration.
Since
\mathscr{C}
admits pushouts, it is also a cocartesian fibration, and applying the straightening construction (cf.
[
44
, Section 3.2]
) gives a functor

\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\widehat{\Cat}_{\infty},\,A\mapsto\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A}.

to the (very large)
\infty
-category
\widehat{\Cat}_{\infty}
of not necessarily small
\infty
-categories.

By
[
44
, Corollary 5.2.2.5]
, this functor factors over the (non-full) subcategory
\PrL\subset\widehat{\Cat}_{\infty}
of presentable
\infty
-categories and colimit-preserving functors.

2.3.
Modules over animated rings

Let
\Sp
be the symmetric monoidal

\infty
-category of spectra
\Sp
with the smash product
\otimes
and its canonical
t
-structure
[
43
]
.
Write
\CAlg
for the
\infty
-category of algebra objects in
\Sp
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra. For
A\in\CAlg
, we write
\Mod_{A}
for the
\infty
-category of
A
-module spectra.

Every ring gives an
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectrum;
the resulting functor
\Poly\rightarrow\CAlg
extends to a sifted-colimit-preserving functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\CAlg,A\mapsto A^{\circ}
.

If
A\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
, we write
\Mod_{A}
for the
\infty
-category of module spectra over
A^{\circ}
. We denote by
\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}_{A}\subset\Mod_{A}
the full subcategory of connective
A
-modules, and by
\Mod^{\heartsuit}_{A}
the full subcategory of discrete
A
-modules.

Remark 2.7
.

If
A
is an ordinary ring, the homotopy category of
\Mod_{A}
is the usual derived category of
A
.
Writing
\Mod_{A}^{\ff}
for the full subcategory of finite free
A
-modules
A^{\oplus n}
, the functor
\Mod_{A}^{\ff}\rightarrow\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}_{A}
gives an equivalence

\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\Mod_{A}^{\ff})\simeq\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}_{A}.

We briefly recall the formal construction of

\infty
-categories of pairs
(A,M)
of associative algebra object
A
and left
A
-module object
M
. The details will be particularly relevant
in our discussion of sheaves of modules in
Section

2.6
below.

Construction 2.8

(Construction of module
\infty
-categories)
.

Assume we are given a symmetric monoidal structure on the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
, i.e. a cocartesian fibration
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\Fin_{\ast}
satisfying the Segal condition (cf.
[
43
, Definition 2.0.0.7]
) with
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}_{\langle 1\rangle}=\mathscr{C}
.

There is a canonical functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Fin_{\ast}
sending
[n]=\{0<\ldots<n\}
to
\langle n\rangle=\{\ast,1,\ldots n\}
, where we think of
i\in\langle n\rangle
as the
i^{th}
inequality sign in
[n]
. Note that a map of ordered sets induces a map on inequality signs in the reverse direction.

Pulling back
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\Fin_{\ast}
along this map gives a monoidal
\infty
-category

\mathscr{C}^{\circledast}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}

(cf.
[
38
, Definition 1.1.2]
).
By Section 2.1 of [op.cit],
\mathscr{C}
is left-tensored over itself, which is formalised by a categorical fibration
\mathscr{C}^{\circledast,L}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\circledast}
satisfying various properties.
Definition 2.1.4 in [op.cit]
then gives a module
\infty
-category

\Mod(\mathscr{C})\subset\Fun_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C}^{\circledast,L}).

By
[
38
, Definition 2.3.3]
, the forgetful functor
\Mod(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\Alg(\mathscr{C})
is a cartesian fibration, giving rise to a functor
\Alg(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\widehat{\Cat}_{\infty}
sending an associative algebra object
A
to
\Mod_{A}
and a map
f:A\rightarrow B
to
f^{\ast}:\Mod_{B}\rightarrow\Mod_{A}
.

Objects in
\Mod(\mathscr{C})
are pairs
(A,M)
of associative algebra object
A\in\Alg(\mathscr{C})
and left
A
-modules
M\in\Mod_{A}
. To give a morphism
(A,M)\rightarrow(B,N)
, we must specify a map of associative algebras
f:A\rightarrow B
and a map of
A
-modules
M\rightarrow f^{\ast}N
.

Remark 2.9
.

In the proof of
Proposition

2.28
below, it will be
helpful to directly realise
\Mod(\mathscr{C})
as an
\infty
-category of algebras over an
\infty
-operad. To this end,
[
43
, Section 4.2.1]
introduces the
\infty
-operad of left modules
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}^{\otimes}
. The left-tensored structure on
\mathscr{C}
gives rise to
a cocartesian fibration
\mathscr{C}^{\circledcirc}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}^{\otimes}
, and
\Mod(\mathscr{C})
is equivalent to
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}^{\otimes}
-algebras in
\mathscr{C}
, i.e. operadic sections of
\mathscr{C}^{\circledcirc}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}^{\otimes}
.

Definition 2.10
.

Define the
\infty
-category of pairs
(A,M)
with
A
an animated ring and
M
an object in
\Mod_{A}
as

\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod:=\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\times_{\Alg(\Sp)}\Mod(\Sp).

Let
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
be the full subcategory of those
(A,M)
with
M
connective.

The
\infty
-category
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
can alternatively be described as follows. Let
\PolyMod^{\ff}
be the ordinary category of pairs
(A,M)
with
A
a polynomial ring and
M
a finite free
A
-module. A morphism in
\PolyMod^{\ff}
is a pair consisting of a map of rings
f\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
, and a map of
A
-modules
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
(or equivalently a map of
A^{\prime}
-modules
A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
). Then by
[
45
, 25.2.1.2]
, we have an equivalence

\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\PolyMod^{\ff})\simeq\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}.

The full subcategory
(\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}})^{\heartsuit}
of discrete objects is equivalent to the ordinary category of pairs
(A,M)
of a discrete ring
A
and a discrete
A
-module
M
.

2.4.
Cotangent complex

We briefly review the construction of André–Quillen’s cotangent complex formalism, referring to

[
45
, Section 25.3]
for a more extensive treatment in the language of
\infty
-categories.

Write
\ArrPoly\subset\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CRing)
for the full subcategory of arrows of the form

\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}].

By
[
47
, Corollary 2.13]
, the functor
\ArrPoly\rightarrow\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
gives
an equivalence

\mathscr{P}_{\Sigma}(\ArrPoly)\simeq\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}).

Definition 2.11

(Cotangent complex)
.

The sifted-colimit-preserving functor

\underline{L}\colon\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}

maps

A\rightarrow B
in
\ArrPoly
to the pair
(B,\Omega^{1}_{B/A})
.

Notation 2.12
.

The composite

\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})\overset{\underline{L}}{\longrightarrow}\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod\longrightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}

is equivalent to the functor mapping
A\rightarrow B
to
B
; this is clear on compact projective generators and extends under sifted colimits.
Hence, we can
write
\underline{L}_{B/A}=:(B,L_{B/A}).

Definition 2.13

(Trivial square-zero extension)
.

The functor

\underline{\sqz}\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}\rightarrow\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})

is the sifted-colimit-preserving extension of the functor mapping
(R,M)
in
\PolyMod^{\ff}
to
R\rightarrow R\oplus M
, where
R\oplus M
is the ring with
(r,x)(s,y)=(rs,ry+sx)
.

We write
\underline{\sqz}(R,M)=(R\rightarrow\sqz_{R}M)
. Note that there is a natural augmentation map
\epsilon:\sqz_{R}(M)\rightarrow R
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
, functorial in
(R,M)
, obtained by applying
\sqz_{R}
to the morphism
M\rightarrow 0
. It preserves sifted colimits and is given by the evident augmentation for objects in
\PolyMod^{\ff}
.

Proposition 2.14
.

The functor
\underline{\sqz}
is right adjoint to
\underline{L}
.

Proof.

This is well-known. We include a proof that will serve as a model for the proof of
Proposition

4.33
, which gives an analogous result for divided power rings.

By
[
44
, Proposition 5.2.2.8]
, it suffices to construct a
unit transformation

\underline{u}:\id\rightarrow\underline{\sqz}\circ\underline{L}.

Note that every map of rings
A\rightarrow B
sits in a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqz_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota+d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
\iota\colon B\rightarrow\sqz_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})=B\oplus\Omega^{1}_{B/A}
is the inclusion and
d
is the universal derivation.
For
A\rightarrow B
in
\ArrPoly
, the vertical maps
define a functorial morphism

\underline{u}:(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow\underline{\sqz}(\underline{L}_{B/A}).

Extending by sifted colimits, we obtain a transformation

\underline{u}:\id\rightarrow\underline{\sqz}\circ\underline{L}.

To verify this is a unit transformation, we check that for
A\rightarrow B
in
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
and
(R,M)
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
, the following composite is an equivalence:

\varphi:\Map\big(\underline{L}_{B/A},\,(R,M)\big)\xrightarrow{\underline{u}\circ\underline{\sqz}}\Map\big((A\rightarrow B),\,\underline{\sqz}(R,M)\big).

First, assume that
A\rightarrow B
is of the form

\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}].

Then
L_{B/A}
is the free
B
-module on generators
dy_{i}
. If
(R,M)\in\PolyMod^{\ff}
, then the map
\varphi
identifies with a map of discrete spaces

R^{n+m}\times M^{m}\rightarrow R^{n}\times(R\oplus M)^{m}
.
Unravelling the definitions, we see that
\varphi
is given by the obvious bijection.

Writing
(R,M)
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
as a sifted colimit of objects in
\PolyMod^{\ff}
, we see that
\phi
is an equivalence since both
A\rightarrow B
and
\underline{L}_{B/A}
are compact projective.

Finally, we can write a general
A\rightarrow B
in
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
as a sifted colimit of morphisms in
\ArrPoly
which shows that
\varphi
is an equivalence in general.
∎

Notation 2.15
.

Denote the counit of the above adjunction by
\underline{v}:\underline{L}\circ\underline{\sqz}\rightarrow\id
.

Remark 2.16
.

The first component of the counit
(\sqz_{B}(M),L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/B})\xrightarrow{\underline{v}}(B,M)
on some
(B,M)\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}

is given by the augmentation
\epsilon:\sqz_{B}(M)\rightarrow B
.

To prove this, let us observe that the two functors
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{1}:\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
restrict to equivalences between
the full subcategory of
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
spanned by all
(B,M)
with
M\simeq 0
, respectively the full subcategory of
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
spanned by all equivalences, and
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Both
\underline{L}
and
\underline{\sqz}
are equivalent to the identity on these two subcategories, and we deduce that the counit on an object
(B,0)
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}
is given by
\id_{(B,0)}
. Given a general object
(B,M)
, we use the naturality of the counit for the morphism
(B,M)\rightarrow(B,0)
to deduce that the first component of the counit
\underline{v}:(\sqz_{B}(M),L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/B})\rightarrow(B,M)
is given by the augmentation
\epsilon:\sqz_{B}(M)\rightarrow B
.

Notation 2.17
.

We write
v:L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/B}\rightarrow\epsilon^{\ast}M
for the map in
\Mod_{\sqz_{B}(M)}
induced by the second component of the counit.

Corollary 2.18
.

Given
A\rightarrow B
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
and
M\in\Mod_{B}^{\mathrm{cn}}
, there is an equivalence

\Map_{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A//B}}(B,\,\sqz_{B}(M))\simeq\Map_{B}(L_{B/A},\,M)

sending

\phi:L_{B/A}\rightarrow M
to the composite
B\xrightarrow{\id\oplus d}\sqz_{B}(L_{B/A})\xrightarrow{\sqz(\phi)}\sqz_{B}(M)
and
sending
s:B\rightarrow\sqz_{B}(M)
to

L_{B/A}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/A}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/B}\rightarrow s^{\ast}\epsilon^{\ast}M\simeq M
.

Proof.

Let us denote the forgetful functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
by
F
.

As in
Notation

2.12
, the composite

\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})\overset{\underline{L}}{\rightarrow}\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}\overset{F}{\rightarrow}\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}

maps
A\rightarrow B
to
B
. By
Remark

2.16
, the map

(F\circ\underline{L}\circ\underline{\sqz})(B,M)\rightarrow F(B,M)

induced by the counit of adjunction is given by the augmentation
\epsilon:\sqz_{B}(M)\rightarrow B
.

We obtain a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})}\big(A\rightarrow B,\,\underline{\sqz}(B,M)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}}\big(\underline{L}_{B/A},\,(B,M)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}}\big(B,B\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the horizontal map is the adjunction equivalence, the right diagonal is induced by
F
, and the left diagonal map is induced by applying
\mathrm{ev}_{1}
and then composing with the augmentation

\epsilon:\sqz_{B}(M)\rightarrow B
.

Passing to fibres of the diagonal maps over
\id_{B}
, we obtain the desired equivalence.
Unravelling the definitions gives the explicit descriptions, using that for every morphism in
\Map_{\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})}\big(A\rightarrow B,\,\underline{\sqz}(B,M)\big)
, the induced map of
B
-modules
L_{B/A}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/B}
factors canonically through

L_{B/A}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{B}(M)/A}
.
∎

We record several well-known properties of this construction (cf.
[
55
,
56
]
).

Proposition 2.19

(Transitivity sequence)
.

Given a sequence of animated rings
A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
, there is a cofibre sequence
C\otimes_{B}L_{B/A}\rightarrow L_{C/A}\rightarrow L_{C/B}.

Proof.

As
\underline{L}:\Fun(\Delta^{1},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod
preserves small colimits, this follows by applying it to the pushout of

(A\rightarrow C)\leftarrow(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(B\xrightarrow{\id}B).

∎

Proposition 2.20

(Base change)
.

Let
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
be a map in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}

and
B\rightarrow C
a map in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A}
. There is a natural equivalence

A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}L_{C/B}\xrightarrow{\simeq}L_{A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}C/A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}B}.

Proof.

This follows by applying the colimit-preserving functor
\underline{L}
to the pushout of the arrows
(B\rightarrow C)\leftarrow(A\xrightarrow{\id}A)\rightarrow(A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\id}A^{\prime}).

∎

Proposition 2.21

(Localisation)
.

Given
A\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
and
f\in\pi_{0}(A)
, we have
L_{A[f^{-1}]/A}=0,
where

A[f^{-1}]=A\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[t]}\mathbf{Z}[t^{\pm 1}]
and
\mathbf{Z}[t]\rightarrow A
sends
t
to
f
.

Proof.

For any
A[f^{-1}]
-module
M
, we have an equivalence

A_{1}[f^{-1}]\otimes_{A}M\simeq M
.
Hence
L_{A[f^{-1}]/A}\simeq A\left[f^{-1}\right]\otimes_{A}L_{A[f^{-1}]/A}
. By
Proposition

2.20
, is is equivalent to

L_{(A[f^{-1}]\otimes_{A}A[f^{-1}])/A\left[f^{-1}\right]}\simeq L_{A[f^{-1}]/A[f^{-1}]}\simeq 0.

∎

2.5.
Sheaves with values in an
\infty
-category

We briefly review the theory of sheaves on sites, (cf.
[
1
, Tome 1, Exposé II]
,
[
44
, Definition 6.2.2.1]
). The only examples strictly needed for our proofs are the small Zariski site
X_{\mathrm{zar}}
and the small étale site
X_{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}
associated to a (classical) scheme
X
.

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a small
\infty
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology (cf.
[
44
, Section 6.2.2]
).
Given an
\infty
-category

\mathscr{C}
with small limits, let

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\subset\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})

be the full subcategory consisting of the
\mathscr{C}
-valued sheaves on
\mathscr{T}
, see
[
45
, Definition 1.3.1.1]
. By
[
45
, Corollary 1.3.1.8]
, there is an equivalence

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\simeq\Fun^{\mathrm{R}}(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}},\,\mathscr{C}).

Here
\Fun^{\mathrm{R}}\subset\Fun
is the full subcategory of functors which preserve small
limits.
Postcomposition with any
F\in\Fun^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
gives a functor
F\colon\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D})
.

By
[
43
, Proposition 4.8.1.15]
,
\infty
-category
\Pr^{\rL}
of presentable
\infty
-categories and left adjoints admits a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
, the Lurie tensor product. We can use
\otimes
to relate
\mathscr{C}
-valued sheaves and sheaves of spaces:

Proposition 2.22
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is presentable then

(1)

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})
is presentable;

(2)

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\simeq\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})\otimes\mathscr{C}
;

(3)

the inclusion
\iota_{\mathscr{C}}:\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
admits a left adjoint, which is called

sheafification
and denoted by
\mathscr{F}\mapsto\mathscr{F}^{\#}
.

Proof.

By
[
44
, Proposition 6.2.2.7]
the
\infty
-category
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})
is presentable, and
[
44
, Remark 5.5.2.10]
shows that
\Fun^{\mathrm{R}}(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}},\,\mathscr{C})
coincides with the full subcategory of functors admitting a left adjoint.
By
[
43
, Lemma 4.8.1.16]
, the
\infty
-category
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\simeq\Fun^{\mathrm{R}}(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}},\,\mathscr{C})
is therefore presentable, and by
[
43
, Proposition 4.8.1.17]
, it is equivalent to
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})\otimes\mathscr{C}
. The third statement then follows as the inclusion
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
admits a left adjoint.
∎

Given a general functor

F\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
(not necessarily preserving small limits) with
\mathscr{D}
presentable, we obtain an induced functor

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\subset\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})\overset{F}{\rightarrow}\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})\overset{\#}{\rightarrow}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D}),

which we will abusively denote by
F\colon\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D})
.

Construction 2.23
.

Given an adjoint pair
L\colon\mathscr{C}\leftrightarrows\mathscr{D}:\!R

between presentable
\infty
-categories. Postcomposing with
R
preserves sheaves, and we obtain adjoint squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathscr{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathscr{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\circ}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\circ-)^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\#}}

Note that the counit
L\circ R\rightarrow\id_{\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})}
of the adjunction on presheaves induces a counit transformation
(-)^{\#}\circ L\circ R\rightarrow\id_{\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D})}

for the adjunction on sheaves.

We abuse notation and denote the resulting adjunction by

L\colon\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{C})\leftrightarrows\Shv(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{D}):\!R

In fact, it follows from
Proposition

2.22
that
\Shv(\mathscr{T},-)
defines a functor
\PrL\rightarrow\PrL
.

For
X
a topological space, write
\mathscr{U}(X)
for the site of open subsets of
X
. By
[
45
, Proposition 1.1.4.4]
, we can characterise sheaves on
X
in terms of compact bases:

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
\mathscr{U}_{e}\subset\mathscr{U}(X)
be a basis for the topology of
X
such that
\mathscr{U}_{e}
is closed under finite intersections and every open
U\in\mathscr{U}_{e}
is compact.

Then
\mathcal{F}:\mathscr{U}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathscr{C}
-valued sheaf
if and only if

(1)

\mathcal{F}
is right Kan extended from
\mathscr{U}_{e}
and

(2)

for all finite collections
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
of open subsets in
\mathscr{U}_{e}
with
\cup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\in\mathscr{U}_{e}
, the following map is an equivalence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(\cup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)\rightarrow\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mathcal{F}\left(\cap_{i\in S}U_{i}\right).

Notation 2.25
.

We write
\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})=\Fun(\mathscr{U}_{e},\mathscr{C})
and
\Shv_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})\subset\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})
for the full subcategory spanned by all functors satisfying condition (
2
) above.

Writing
\Ran:\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,\mathscr{C})
for the right Kan extension and
(-)^{\#}_{e}
for the adjoint of the inclusion
\Shv_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})
, we obtain by

Proposition

2.24
:

Corollary 2.26
.

In the setting of
Proposition

2.24
, there are adjoint squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(X,\mathscr{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\mathscr{C}}_{X,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\mathscr{C}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ran}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{e}(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X,\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{e}(X,\mathscr{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ (-)^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ (-)^{\#}_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

2.6.
Sheaves of modules

We again fix a small
\infty
-category
\mathscr{T}
equipped with a Grothendieck topology.
The
\infty
-category of presheaves
\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)
is equipped with the pointwise smash product. By
[
43
, Proposition 2.2.1.9]
, the
\infty
-category
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)
of sheaves of spectra
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure making the sheafification functor
\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)\rightarrow\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)
symmetric monoidal.

Proposition 2.27
.

There is a canonical equivalence

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg)\simeq\CAlg(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp))

between sheaves of
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra and commutative algebras in sheaves of spectra.

Proof.

Currying gives an equivalence (in fact isomorphism) of
\infty
-categories

\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\CAlg)=\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Fun_{\Fin_{\ast}}^{\oper}(\Fin_{\ast},\Sp^{\otimes}))

\ \ \simeq\Fun_{\Fin_{\ast}}^{\oper}(\Fin_{\ast},\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)^{\otimes})=\CAlg(\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)),

where
\Fun^{{\oper}}
denotes
maps of
\infty
-operads and

\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)^{\otimes}
encodes the
pointwise symmetric monoidal structure on
\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)
.

As the forgetful functor
\CAlg\rightarrow\Sp
is conservative and preserves small limits (cf.
[
43
, Section 3.2.2]
), the above equivalence identifies
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg)
with the full subcategory of
\Fun_{\Fin_{\ast}}^{\oper}(\Fin_{\ast},\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)^{\otimes})
spanned by all functors sending
\langle 1\rangle
to a sheaf of spectra. This is equivalent to
\CAlg(\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp))
as

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)^{\otimes}\rightarrow\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)^{\otimes}
is a map of
\infty
-operads by
[
43
, Proposition 2.2.1.9 (3)]
.

∎

If
\mathcal{A}\in\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
is a sheaf of animated rings, we let
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\in\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg)
be the underlying sheaf of
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-rings. It is obtained by
first postcomposing with the forgetful functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\CAlg

and then sheafifying (cf.
[
45
, Proposition 1.3.5.10]
).
By
Proposition

2.27
, we can interpret
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
as an
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)
.

Using
Construction

2.8
, we define

\Mod(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A}):=\Mod_{\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp))=\{\mathcal{A}\}\times_{\Alg(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp))}\Mod(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)).

This is a presentable stable
\infty
-category by
[
43
, Theorem 3.4.4.2]
. Write
\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})
for the full subcategory spanned by those
\mathcal{M}\in\Mod(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})
such that the homotopy sheaves
\pi_{n}\mathcal{M}\in\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Ab)
vanish for all
n<0
. This forms the connective part of a
t
-structure on the stable
\infty
-category
\Mod(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})
by
[
45
, Proposition 2.1.1.1]
.

Using
Definition

2.10
, we can give a more ‘algebraic’ definition:

Proposition 2.28
.

There are canonical equivalences

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{A}\}\times_{\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\Mod(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{A}\}\times_{\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A}),

Proof.

Using the notation from
Construction

2.8
, we note that by
[
43
, Proposition 2.2.1.9(3)]
, the inclusion
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)^{\circledcirc}\rightarrow\Fun(\mathscr{T}^{\mathrm{op}},\Sp)^{\circledcirc}
is a map of
\infty
-operads over
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}^{\otimes}
. We can therefore conclude as in

Proposition

2.27
that there is an equivalence

\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Mod(\Sp))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Mod(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp)),

which sits in a commutative square consisting of forgetful functors and the equivalence
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Alg(\Sp))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Alg(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp))
.
Passing to the fibres over the respective image of
\mathcal{A}
in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Alg(\Sp))
and
\Alg(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp))
gives the first equivalence.

Replacing
\Sp
by
\Sp^{\mathrm{cn}}
in the above argument gives rise to an equivalence

\{\mathcal{A}\}\times_{\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Alg(\Sp^{\mathrm{cn}}))}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Mod(\Sp^{\mathrm{cn}}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\{\mathcal{A}\}\times_{\Alg(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp^{\mathrm{cn}}))}\Mod(\Shv(\mathscr{T},\Sp^{\mathrm{cn}})).

The right hand side is equivalent to
\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})
by
[
45
, Proposition 1.3.5.8]
.
∎

Notation 2.29
.

We will write objects in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}})
as pairs
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{M})
where
\mathcal{A}\in\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
is the image under the forgetful functor and
\mathcal{M}\in\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{A})
is the image under the second equivalence in

Proposition

2.28
.

Warning 2.30
.

Even for
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
a classical scheme,
\Mod(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is generally
not
equivalent to
\mathscr{D}(\Mod_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\heartsuit})
, the derived
\infty
-category of the classical abelian category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, as the latter only contains the hypercomplete sheaves.

This distinction disappears for example when
X
is quasi-compact, quasi-separated, and of finite Krull dimension (cf.
[
45
, Theorem 2.1.2.2.]
[
12
, Theorem 3.12]
).

2.7.
Cotangent complex for sheaves of animated rings

To extend the cotangent complex to maps of sheaves of animated rings, note that
by §
2.5
, the adjoint pair
(\underline{L},\,\underline{\sqz})
of
Proposition

2.14
induces an adjunction

\underline{L}:\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}))\leftrightarrows\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}):\underline{\sqz}.

The composite

\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}))\overset{\underline{L}}{\rightarrow}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}})\overset{F}{\rightarrow}\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})

maps
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{B}
, and we can write
\underline{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{B},L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}})
with
L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\in\Mod_{\mathcal{B}}
. Similarly, we write
\underline{\sqz}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M})=(\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{M})
.

We denote the counit of this adjunction by
\underline{v}\colon\underline{L}\circ\underline{\sqz}\rightarrow\id
.
Given some
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M})
in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}})
, the counit
(\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{M},L_{\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M})

is obtained by
sheafifying
the domain of the counit of the corresponding adjunction of presheaves. We deduce from the
Remark

2.16

that the ‘first component’ of
\underline{v}
is the canonical augmentation
\epsilon:\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(0)\simeq\mathcal{B}
, and the second component gives a map
v\colon L_{\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow\epsilon^{\ast}\mathcal{M}
. We can now sheafify
Corollary

2.18
:

Proposition 2.31
.

Given a map
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
and
\mathcal{M}\in\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}_{\mathcal{B}}
, there is an equivalence

\Map_{\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})_{\mathcal{A}//\mathcal{B}}}\big(\mathcal{B},\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})\big)\simeq\Map_{{\mathcal{B}}}(L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}},\,\mathcal{M})

sending

\phi:L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
to the composite
\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{\id\oplus d}\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{\sqz(\phi)}\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})
and

s:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})
to

L_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})/\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow s^{\ast}L_{\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})/\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow s^{\ast}\epsilon^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Corollary

2.18
,
we obtain a
commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map\big(\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B},\,\underline{\sqz}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map\big(\,\underline{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}},\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map\big(\,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

in which the horizontal map is the adjunction equivalence, and the diagonal maps are induced by the map
\sqz_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
and by
F
respectively. Considering fibres over
\id_{\mathcal{B}}
then proves the proposition.
∎

Notation 2.32
.

Let
A
be an animated ring. Given a pair
X=(\mathscr{T},\mathcal{O}_{X})
of a Grothendieck site
\mathscr{T}
and a sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
in
\Shv(\mathscr{T},\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{A})
, we write
L_{X/A}:=L_{\mathcal{O}_{X}/A}.

2.8.
Cotangent complex for derived schemes

We will now check that
Definition

2.11
and
Notation

2.32
are compatible with each other.
Let us write
\Topp_{{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}}}
for the
\infty
-category of
animated ringed spaces
, using
[
45
, Construction 1.1.2.2]
.
Its objects are given by pairs
(X\in\Topp,\ \mathcal{O}_{X}\in\Shv(X,{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}}))
and morphisms
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
are
given by pairs

(f:X\xrightarrow{}Y\ ,\ \phi:\mathcal{O}_{Y}\xrightarrow{}f_{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{X}))
.

Definition 2.33

(Derived schemes)
.

A
derived scheme
is a pair
X=(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
with
X
a topological space and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
an object in
\Shv(X,\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
such that

(1)

(X,\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a scheme;

(2)

the
\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\pi_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is quasi-coherent for all
n\geq 0
;

(3)

\mathcal{O}_{X}
is hypercomplete.

A morphism
X\rightarrow Y
of derived schemes is a pair consisting of a continuous map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
and a map
\phi:\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
in
\Shv(X,\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}})
such that the induced map

(X,\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow(Y,\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{Y})
is a morphism of schemes.

The
\infty
-category of derived schemes is the subcategory
\Sch^{\der}\subset\Topp_{{\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}}}
of derived schemes and maps of derived schemes between them.

Remark 2.34
.

If
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
satisfies (1) and (2) in
Definition

2.33
, then (3) is equivalent to the assertion that for every affine open
U\subset(X,\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X})
the natural map
\pi_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(U))\rightarrow(\pi_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X})(U)

is an isomorphism, cf. the proof of
[
45
, Proposition 1.1.6.2]
.

Example 2.35

(Affine derived schemes)
.

Every animated ring
B\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
gives a derived scheme
\Spec(B)=(|\Spec(B)|,\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(B)})
, whose structure sheaf maps a distinguished open
U_{f}=\Spec(\pi_{0}(B)[f^{-1}])
to
B[f^{-1}]

The resulting assignment
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\Sch^{\der}
,
B\mapsto\Spec(B)
is fully faithful.

Remark 2.36
.

Given a derived scheme
X
over an animated ring
A
, we
define
L_{X/A}
following
Notation

2.32
. Note that any map
A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
induces a map
L_{X/A}\rightarrow L_{X/A^{\prime}}
. With a bit more work, one can in fact define
L
as a functor on arrows of animated ringed spaces.

Definition 2.37

(Quasi-coherence)
.

A sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{M}
on a derived scheme
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is
quasi-coherent
if for all inclusions of
affine
open subsets
U\subset V
of
X
, the following canonical morphism is an equivalence:

\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(V)}\mathcal{M}(V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)}\mathcal{M}(U)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(U).

Warning 2.38
.

The assignment
U\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
is usually not a sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, as the forgetful functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\Mod_{\mathbf{Z}}
does not preserve limits. However, its underlying sheaf of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\circ}
(obtained by sheafification) is a quasi-coherent sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, which agrees with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
on affines opens.

Remark 2.39
.

By
[
45
, 2.2.6.1]
, the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is quasi-coherent if and only if it is hypercomplete and all its homotopy sheaves are quasi-coherent
\pi_{0}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.

Proposition 2.40
.

Let
X=\Spec(B)
be an affine derived scheme.
Then a sheaf
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules is quasi-coherent if and only if for all distinguished affine opens
U^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
of
X
, the morphism

\mathcal{O}_{X}(U^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(V^{\prime})}\mathcal{M}(V^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(U^{\prime})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Fix an inclusion of affine open subsets
U\subset V
of
\Spec(B)
.

First assume
V
is already distinguished. We can cover it by finitely many distinguished affine opens
U_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,U_{m}^{\prime}
.
Proceeding as in
[
45
, Proposition 1.1.4.4]
,
we see that the natural map
\mathcal{M}(U)\rightarrow\lim_{\emptyset\neq S\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}}\mathcal{M}(U_{S}^{\prime})
is an equivalence, where
U_{S}^{\prime}=\bigcap_{i\in S}U^{\prime}_{i}
, and a similar expression holds for
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
.
As tensor products commute with finite limits, we can write

\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(V)}\mathcal{M}(V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(U)

as a limit of maps

\mathcal{O}_{X}(U_{i}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(V)}\mathcal{M}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(U_{i}^{\prime})
, which shows the result for
V
distinguished.

We cover general inclusions of affines by finitely many distinguished affines.
∎

Proposition 2.41
.

Fix a map of animated rings
q:A\longrightarrow B
. Then
L_{\Spec(B)/A}
(cf.
Notation

2.32
) is the quasi-coherent sheaf on
\Spec(B)
with global sections

L_{B/A}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{O}_{R}=\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(R)}
for
R\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
.
First, note that for any open
V\subset\Spec(A)
, we have

L_{\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)/A}\simeq 0
.

Indeed, we can cover
V
by distinguished affines
U_{f}\subset V
, and it
suffices to check that for any such
U_{f}
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{A}(U_{f})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)}L_{\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)/A}=0
.
This follows from
Proposition

2.19
,

Proposition

2.21
, and the equivalence
\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)[f^{-1}]\simeq A[f^{-1}]
, which we can show using the limit cube for
\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)
induced by the distinguished
cover of
V
.

Given
U\subset\Spec(B)
open,

(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U)
is a filtered colimit of animated rings
\mathcal{O}_{A}(V)
, where
V
is an open containing
q(U)
.
Hence
L_{(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U)/A}=0
,
and
Proposition

2.19
gives an equivalence

L_{\mathcal{O}_{B}(U)/A}\xrightarrow{\simeq}L_{\mathcal{O}_{B}(U)/(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U)}.

Together with Propositions
2.21
and
2.19
, this shows that for any inclusion
U_{f}\subset U_{g}
of distinguished affines, the following square consists of equivalences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[f^{-1}]\otimes_{B[g^{-1}]}L_{B[g^{-1}]/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{B[f^{-1}]/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{B}(U_{f})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{B}(U_{g})}L_{\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(B)}(U_{g})/(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U_{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(B)}(U_{f})/(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U_{f})}.}

Let
\mathscr{U}_{e}
be the poset of distinguished open subsets of
\Spec(B)
.
Using the above formula for
g=1
, we see that the restriction of the presheaf
U\mapsto\underline{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{B}(U_{f})/(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U_{f})}
to
\mathscr{U}_{e}

satisfies
Proposition

2.24
(2).

Corollary

2.26
shows that sheafification preserves the values on distinguished affines
U_{f}\subset\Spec(B)
, so we obtain:

\underline{L}_{\Spec(B)/\Spec(A)}(U_{f})\simeq(\mathcal{O}_{B}(U_{f}),{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{B}(U_{f})/(q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{A})(U_{f})})\simeq(B[f^{-1}],{L}_{B[f^{-1}]/A}).

Proposition

2.40
now implies that
{L}_{\Spec(B)/\Spec(A)}
is quasi-coherent.
∎

Corollary 2.42

(Quasi-coherence of
L_{X/A}
)
.

Given a derived scheme
X
over an animated ring
A
,
the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module

L_{X/A}
is quasi-coherent.

Proof.

As the assertion is local on
X
, this follows from
Proposition

2.41
.
∎

Remark 2.43
.

In particular, this shows that
L_{X/A}
is hypercomplete.
For
X
a classical scheme, we deduce that
L_{X/A}
belongs to
\mathscr{D}(\Mod_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\heartsuit})
, and it is not hard to verify that it agrees with the classical cotangent complex of
X
.

We will compare the cotangent complex in the Zariski and the étale site.
Let
X
be a derived scheme over
A\in\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}
. Fix an uncountably strong
limit cardinal
\kappa>|X|
.

Definition 2.44

(Small étale site)
.

The
small étale site

X_{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}
consists of all
maps of derived schemes
U\rightarrow X
with
|U|<\kappa
which are

étale
, i.e. locally obtained by applying
\Spec(-)
to an étale map of animated rings (see
[
37
, Definition 3.4.7]
).
The coverings of this site are jointly surjective families.

Let us temporarily denote
the étale structure sheaf of
X
by

\mathcal{O}_{X,\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}
. Given an étale open
(U\rightarrow X)
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{X,\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}(U\rightarrow X)\simeq\mathcal{O}_{U}(U)
.
Every
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module

\mathcal{F}
on
X
gives an étale sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X,\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}
-modules

\iota^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
on
X_{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}
, by sheafifying the presheaf

(U\xrightarrow{h}X)\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{U}(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(h(U))}\mathcal{F}(h(U)).

Notation

2.32
gives a priori different cotangent complexes
L^{\mathrm{zar}}_{X/A}
and
L^{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}_{X/A}
for the Zariski and étale topology.

Proposition 2.45
.

There is a canonical equivalence
i^{\ast}L_{X/A}^{\mathrm{zar}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}L_{X/A}^{\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}.

Proof.

For any étale open
h:U\rightarrow X
, the pullback map
\mathcal{O}_{X}(h(U))\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{U}(U)
is étale, so the canonical map

\mathcal{O}_{X,\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(h(U))}L_{\mathcal{O}_{X}(h(U))/A}\rightarrow L_{\mathcal{O}_{X,\mathrm{\text{\'{e}t}}}(U)/A}

is an equivalence by
Proposition

2.19
. The claim follows by sheafifying.
∎

3.
Formal moduli problems

In this section, we will review the basics of derived deformation theory, and introduce several examples of formal moduli problems along the way.

3.1.
Artinian animated rings

Fix a local Noetherian ring
\Lambda
with
residue field
k
.

Definition 3.1

(Artinian animated
\Lambda
-algebras)
.

An object
A
of
\CAlg_{\Lambda//k}^{\mathrm{an}}
is said to be
Artinian
if

(1)

\pi_{0}(A)
is a local Artinian ring with residue field
k
;

(2)

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\pi_{i}(A)
is a
\pi_{0}(A)
-module of finite length.

Let
\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}\subset\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda//k}
be the full subcategory spanned by all Artinian objects.

If
A\rightarrow B
is a map in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}
with fibre
J
, then the
\Lambda
-module
\oplus_{i}\pi_{i}(J)
has finite length. We denote its length by
\len(A\rightarrow B)
.
Recall the following notion:

Definition 3.2

(Discrete elementary extension)
.

Let
A\rightarrow B
be a map of discrete rings in
\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}
. Denote the maximal ideal of
A
by
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
and set
I=\ker(A\rightarrow B)
. We say that
A\rightarrow B
is a
elementary extension
if
A\rightarrow B
is surjective and
\mathfrak{m}_{A}I=0
.

The following two statements are well known
(
[
37
, Lemma 6.2.6]

[
21
, Lemma 2.8]
):

Lemma 3.3
.

Given a map
A\rightarrow B
in
\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}
which is not an equivalence and such that
\pi_{0}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(B)
is surjective, there is a finite-dimensional
k
-vector space
V
, an integer
n\geq 0
,
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqz_{k}(V[n+1])}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}
such that

(1)

the square is a pullback square;

(2)

\pi_{0}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(B^{\prime})
is surjective;

(3)

\len(A\rightarrow B^{\prime})<\len(A\rightarrow B)
.

If
A\rightarrow B
is a map of discrete rings, then one can take
n=0
and
B^{\prime}
discrete. If
A\rightarrow B
is moreover an elementary extension, then one can take
B^{\prime}=A
.

Proof.

Let us denote the (connective) fibre of
A\rightarrow B
by
J
.
Let
n
be the smallest integer such that
\pi_{n}(J)
is non-zero. The connectivity estimate of
[
45
, Proposition 25.3.6.1]
shows that the natural map
B\otimes_{A}J[1]\rightarrow L_{B/A}
induces an isomorphism

M[n+1]:=\left(\pi_{0}(B)\boldsymbol{\otimes}_{\pi_{0}(A)}\pi_{n}(J)\right)[n+1]\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\tau_{\leq n+1}L_{B/A},

where
\boldsymbol{\otimes}
denotes the underived tensor product.
The Artinian
\pi_{0}(B)
-module
M
admits a nonzero surjective
\pi_{0}(B)
-linear map
M\rightarrow V
to a
k
-vector space
V
(seen as
\pi_{0}(B)
-module via the augmentation
\pi_{0}(B)\rightarrow k
). The resulting composite

L_{B/A}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n+1}L_{B/A}\simeq M[n+1]\rightarrow V[n+1]

induces a map of animated
A
-algebras
B\rightarrow\sqz_{B}(V[n+1])\rightarrow\sqz_{k}(V[n+1])
. We now define
B^{\prime}
as the pullback of this map against the unit
k\rightarrow\sqz_{k}(V[n+1])
and obtain a diagram as in the statement of the lemma.

Writing
J^{\prime}
for the fibre of
A\rightarrow B^{\prime}
, we have a fibre sequence

J^{\prime}\rightarrow J\rightarrow V[n]

and the map
J\rightarrow V[n]
is surjective on
\pi_{n}
.
It follows that
J^{\prime}
is connective (hence
\pi_{0}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(B^{\prime})
surjective) and that
\len(A\rightarrow B^{\prime})<\len(A\rightarrow B)
. If
A
and
B
are both discrete, then we have
n=0
and it follows that
J^{\prime}
and hence
B^{\prime}
are also both discrete. If moreover
A\rightarrow B
is an elementary extension, then
M
is a
k
-vector space, and so we can take
V=M
and find that
A\rightarrow B^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

For every
A\rightarrow B
in
\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}
with
\pi_{0}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(B)
surjective, there exists a factorisation

A=A_{n}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{1}\rightarrow A_{0}=B

where each of the maps sits in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqz_{k}(V_{i}[n_{i}+1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

with
n_{i}\geq 0
. If
A,B
are discrete, then we can pick
A_{i}
discrete
and
n_{i}=0
for all
i
.

3.2.
Deformation functors and formal moduli problems

Let us denote the ordinary category of local Artinian
\Lambda
-algebras with residue field
k
by

\CRing^{\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}.
We recall
Schlessinger’s classical
definition of a deformation functor from
[
58
]
:

Definition 3.5

(Deformation functors)
.

A
deformation functor
is a functor

F\colon\CRing_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{art}}\rightarrow\Set

such that

(1)

F(k)
has exactly one element;

(2)

for every pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
\CRing_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{art}}
with
A\rightarrow B
surjective, the following induced map is surjective:

\varphi\colon F(A^{\prime})\rightarrow F(A)\times_{F(B)}F(B^{\prime}).

(3)

if in (
2
) we take
B^{\prime}=k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}
and
B=k
, then the map
\varphi
is bijective.

We define a derived version of a deformation functor, following
[
41
,
42
,
11
]
:

Definition 3.6

(Formal moduli problems)
.

A functor

F:\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

is called a
formal moduli problem
if

(1)

F(k)
is contractible;

(2)

for every pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}
for which the maps
\pi_{0}A\rightarrow\pi_{0}B
and
\pi_{0}B^{\prime}\rightarrow\pi_{0}B
are surjective, applying
F
gives a pullback square in
\mathscr{S}
.

We write
\Moduli_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}\subset\Fun(\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}},\mathscr{S})
for the full subcategory spanned by the formal moduli problems.

Lemma 3.7
.

If
F\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is a formal moduli problem, then

\CRing^{\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\overset{F}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{S}\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}\Set

is a deformation functor in the sense of
Definition

3.5
.

Notation 3.8
.

We will denote the deformation functor associated to a formal moduli problem
F
by
F^{\mathrm{cl}}\colon\CRing^{\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\Set
. We can think of
F
as a derived enhancement of the deformation functor
F^{\mathrm{cl}}
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.7
.

The first condition in
Definition

3.5
is immediate. For the second condition, one uses the reasoning of
[
45
, Proposition 12.1.3.7]
to show that
F
in fact satisfies the pullback property in
Definition

3.6
when only
\pi_{0}A\rightarrow\pi_{0}B
is asked to be surjective. For the third condition, note that if
B^{\prime}\rightarrow B
is the map
k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}\rightarrow k
, then
F(B)
is contractible, and we have an equivalence
F(A^{\prime})\simeq F(A)\times F(B^{\prime})
inducing a bijection
F^{\mathrm{cl}}(A^{\prime})\cong F^{\mathrm{cl}}(A)\times F^{\mathrm{cl}}(B^{\prime})
.
∎

In light of
Definition

3.6
,

Corollary

3.4
immediately implies that we can detect equivalences of formal moduli problems on trivial square-zero extensions:

Proposition 3.9

(Conservativity)
.

A map
F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
in
\Moduli_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
is an equivalence if and only if the map

F(\sqz k[n])\rightarrow F^{\prime}(\sqz k[n])

is an equivalence for all
n\geq 0
.

Remark 3.10
.

The analogous statement for classical deformation functors is false: if
F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
is a map of deformation functors inducing an isomorphism of tangent spaces
F(k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2})\rightarrow F^{\prime}(k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2})
, then
F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
need not be an isomorphism.

Remark 3.11

(Tangent fibre)
.

One can construct a functor

T\colon\Moduli_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\Mod_{k},\,F\mapsto T_{F}

such that for all discrete finite-dimensional
k
-modules
V
and all
n\geq 0
, we have

(2)

F(\sqz(V[n]))\simeq\Map_{\Mod_{k}}(V^{\vee}[n],T_{F})\simeq\Omega^{\infty-n}(T_{F}\otimes_{k}V).

Indeed, given a formal moduli problem
F\in\Moduli_{\Lambda}
, the sequence

\left(\ \ \ldots,\ \ F(\sqz_{k}(-^{\vee}[2])),\ \ F(\sqz_{k}(-^{\vee}[1])),\ \ F(\sqz_{k}(-^{\vee}))\right)

defines an object
T_{F}\in\Mod_{k}
– the
tangent fibre
of
F
.

Here we have used that
\Mod_{k}^{\mathrm{cn}}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{Vect}_{k}^{\omega})
, the

\infty
-category of finite-product-preserving presheaves on the category of discrete
finite-dimensional
k
-modules
\mathrm{Vect}_{k}^{\omega}
, and that

\Mod_{k}
arises as the inverse limit of
\infty
-categories

\Mod_{k}\simeq\lim\left(\ldots\rightarrow\Mod_{k}^{\mathrm{cn}}\xrightarrow{\Omega}\Mod_{k}^{\mathrm{cn}}\xrightarrow{\Omega}\Mod_{k}^{\mathrm{cn}}\right),

where the transition functor
\Omega
is given by
M\mapsto\tau_{\geq 0}(M[-1])
.

Proposition

3.9
states that the functor
T
is conservative. With more work, one can show that the above tangent fibre functor can be lifted to an equivalence

\Moduli_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}\overset{\simeq}{\rightarrow}\Lie_{\Lambda/k,\Delta}^{\pi}

between formal moduli problems and
(\Lambda,k)
-partition Lie algebras, cf.
[
11
, Theorem 6.27]
. For
\Lambda=k
a field of characteristic
0
, this was established by Lurie
[
41
]
and Pridham
[
54
]
, building on work of Hinich
[
27
]
, Deligne
[
15
]
,
Drinfel’d
[
17
]
, and others.

3.3.
Obstructions

Let us fix a formal moduli problem
F\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{art},\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
.
By
Lemma

3.3
,
any elementary extension
A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A
of discrete local Artinian
\Lambda
-algebras with residue field
k
arises as a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqz_{k}(I[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
\CAlg_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}
, where
I=\ker(A^{\prime}\rightarrow A)
.
Applying
F
gives a fibre sequence of spaces

F(A^{\prime})\rightarrow F(A)\rightarrow F(\sqz_{k}(I[1])).

Definition 3.12

(Obstruction classes)
.

Given
x\in\pi_{0}F(A)
,
the
obstruction class

\ob_{F}(x,A^{\prime}\rightarrow A)
to lifting
x
to
A^{\prime}
is given
by the image of
x
in
\pi_{0}F(k\oplus I[1])
.

Clearly
x
admits a lift to
A^{\prime}
if and only if
\ob_{F}(x,A^{\prime}\rightarrow A)
vanishes.

We can now introduce a central notion studied in this paper:

Definition 3.13

(Unobstructedness)
.

A deformation functor
F
is
unobstructed
if for all surjections
A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A

in
\CRing_{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{art}}
the map
F(A^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow F(A)
is a surjection.

Since every surjection
A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A
of discrete Artinian
\Lambda
-algebras factors as a composition of elementary extensions (cf.
Corollary

3.4
), we deduce:

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
F\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
be a formal moduli problem. Then
F^{\mathrm{cl}}
is unobstructed if and only if for every elementary extension
A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
in
\CRing^{\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}
and every
x\in\pi_{0}F(A)
, the obstruction class

\ob_{F}(x,A^{\prime}\rightarrow A)
vanishes.

3.4.
Deformations of modules

In the remainder of this section, we will construct several formal moduli problems required to prove our main theorems.

To begin with, we consider derived deformations of modules, following
[
45
, Section 16.5.2]
.
Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\Lambda
-linear stable
\infty
-category, i.e. a left
\Mod_{\Lambda}
-module in
\Pr^{\rL,\st}
, the
\infty
-category of presentable stable
\infty
-categories with the Lurie tensor product. By
[
38
, Remark 4.1.8, Corollary 2.6.6]
, we see that
\mathscr{C}
is left-tensored over
\Mod_{\Lambda}
, and we write
\Mod(\mathscr{C})
for the
\infty
-category of pairs
(A,M)
of associative algebra object
A
in
\Mod_{\Lambda}
and left
A
-modules
M
in
\mathscr{C}
, see [op.cit, Definition 2.1.4].

Let us fix a left
k
-module object
M
in
\Mod_{k}(\mathscr{C})
that we want to deform.

Construction 3.15

(The functor
\Def^{\mathrm{mod}}_{M}
)
.

Consider the
\infty
-category

\mathscr{D}:=\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda}\times_{\Alg_{\Lambda}}\Mod(\mathscr{C}),

of pairs
(A,N)
with
A
in
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}
and
N
a left
A
-module object in
\mathscr{C}
.
The projection
q\colon\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda}
defines a cocartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
sending
A
to
\Mod_{A}({\mathscr{C}})^{\simeq}
, the maximal Kan complex contained in the
\infty
-category
\Mod_{A}({\mathscr{C}})
of left
A
-modules. A map

(A,N)\rightarrow(A^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is cocartesian iff it induces an equivalence
A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}N\xrightarrow{\simeq}N^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{cocart}}
be the
non-full
subcategory of
\mathscr{D}
consisting of all objects and only those morphisms which are
q
-cocartesian.
By
[
44
, Corollary 2.4.2.5]
, the restriction of
q
to
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{cocart}}
is a left fibration, and one readily verifies that
that the induced map

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/(k,M)}\rightarrow\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda//k}

is also a left fibration. Straightening then gives rise to a functor
\CAlg^{\mathrm{an}}_{\Lambda//k}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
, and restricting to Artinian objects defines the functor
\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
.

Remark 3.16

(Informal description)
.

Objects in
\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(A)\in\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
correspond to
pairs consisting of an
A
-module
N
in
\mathscr{C}
and an equivalence
k\otimes_{A}N\xrightarrow{\simeq}M
. The
1
-simplices correspond to compatible equivalences of
A
-modules
N\xrightarrow{\simeq}N^{\prime}
.

Throughout this paper, we will make use of the following useful notation:

Notation 3.17

(
\Map^{\prime}
)
.

Given a map
f\colon y\rightarrow x
in some
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
, we define

\Map^{\prime}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y):=\Map_{\mathscr{C}_{/x}}(x,y)

as the space of sections of
f
, which sits in a
pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\ast\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\mathscr{C}}(x,x).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

The map
f
is implicit in the notation, and should be clear from the context.

Given a functor
F\colon\CAlg^{\mathrm{an},\mathrm{art}}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
, the natural section of
\sqz_{k}(V)\rightarrow k
equips
F(\sqz_{k}(V))
with a base point. Write
\Omega F(\sqz_{k}(V))
for the corresponding loop space.

Note that for
F=\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}
as in
Construction

3.15
, the base point in
\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\sqz_{k}(V))
corresponds to the trivial deformation
\sqz_{k}(V)\otimes_{k}M
of
M
to
\sqz_{k}(V)
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
V\in\Mod^{\mathrm{cn}}_{k}
be perfect. Then there is an equivalence

\Omega\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\sqz_{k}(V))\simeq\Map^{\prime}_{\Mod_{k}(\mathscr{C})}(M,\,\sqz_{k}(V)\otimes_{k}M)

where the implicit map
\sqz_{k}(V)\otimes_{k}M\rightarrow M
is the one induced by tensoring
M
with the canonical augmentation
\sqz_{k}(V)\rightarrow k
.

Proof.

Let us write
A=\sqz_{k}(V)
for brevity. Unravelling the definitions, we see that
\Omega\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(A)
sits in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{{A}(\mathscr{C})^{\simeq}}\big(A\otimes_{k}M,\,A\otimes_{k}M\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\ast\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\Mod_{k}(\mathscr{C})^{\simeq}}(M,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes_{A}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

in
\widehat{S}
. Hence
\Omega\Def_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}(A)
is essentially small, and the above square is equivalent to a pullback square in
\mathscr{S}
. Given
M^{\prime}\in\Mod_{A}(\mathscr{C})
, tensoring over
A
with the fibre sequence
V\rightarrow A\rightarrow k
gives a fibre sequence
